z:=T(x);\ (z^{\prime})^{(\pi)}:=T^{\prime}(x);\ \textbf{assert}(x\in\mathcal{A}_{1});\ \textbf{assert}(z\in\mathcal{A}_{2});\ \textbf{assert}(z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{3}).

Note that after the third substep,
x,z,(z^{\prime})^{(\pi)}
occur in the assignments, but none of
x^{(\pi)},z^{(\pi)}
and
z^{\prime}
do. In the fourth substep, we replace
\textbf{assert}(z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{3})
with
\textbf{assert}((z^{\prime})^{(\pi)}\in\mathcal{A}^{\pi}_{3})
and get the following symbolic execution in
\mathsf{SL}
[
\circ
,
\mathsf{sreplaceAll}
,
\mathsf{reverse}
, FT] where only FTs and regular constraints occur,

z:=T(x);\ (z^{\prime})^{(\pi)}:=T^{\prime}(x);\ \textbf{assert}(x\in\mathcal{A}_{1});\ \textbf{assert}(z\in\mathcal{A}_{2});\ \textbf{assert}((z^{\prime})^{(\pi)}\in\mathcal{A}^{\pi}_{3}).

∎

As mentioned in Section
0.1
, our algorithm
reduces the problem to constraints which can be handled by the
existing solver SLOTH
[
HJLRV18
]
.

Extensions with length constraints.

Apart from regular constraints in the assertion, length constraints are another class of commonly used constraints in string manipulating programs. Some simple length constraints can be encoded by regular constraints, as partially exemplified in Example
0.3.2
. Here, we show that when
\mathsf{SL}
[
\circ
,
\mathsf{sreplaceAll}
,
\mathsf{reverse}
, FT] is extended with (much more) general length constraints, the
expspace
upper bound can still be preserved.
We remark that, in contrast, if
\mathsf{SL}
[
\circ
,
\mathsf{replaceAll}
,
\mathsf{reverse}
, FFT] is extended with length constraints, then the path feasibility problem becomes undecidable, which has already been shown in
[
CCHLW18
]
.

To specify length constraints properly, we need to slightly extend our constraint language. In particular, we consider variables of type
{\sf Int}
, which are usually referred to as
integer variables
and range over the set
\mathbb{N}
of natural numbers. Recall that, in previous sections, we have used
x,y,z,\ldots
to denote the variables of
{\sf Str}
type. Hereafter we typically use
\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{n},\ldots
to denote the variables of
{\sf Int}
. The
choice of omitting negative integers is for simplicity. Our
results can be easily extended to the case where
{\sf Int}
includes negative integers.

Definition 0.5.8

(Length assertions)
.

A length assertion is of the form

\textbf{assert}(a_{1}t_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}t_{n}\leq d)
,
where
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},d\in\mathbb{Z}
are integer constants (represented in binary), and each
term

t_{i}
is either

1.

an integer variable
\mathfrak{n}
;

2.

|x|
where
x
is a string variable; or

3.

|x|_{a}
where
x
is string variable and
a\in\Sigma
is a constant letter.

Here,
|x|
and
|x|_{a}
denote the length of
x
and the number of occurrences of
a
in
x
, respectively.

Theorem 0.5.9
.

The path feasibility of
\mathsf{SL}
[
\circ
,
\mathsf{sreplaceAll}
,
\mathsf{reverse}
, FT] extended with length assertions is
expspace
-complete.

The proof of the theorem is given in Appendix
in the supplementary material.
Other related constraints, such as character assertions and
\mathsf{IndexOf}
assertions, can be encoded by length assertions defined in Definition
0.5.8
together with regular constraints. Since they are not the focus of this paper, we omit the details here.

We observe that Theorem
0.5.9
suggests that the two semantic conditions (roughly speaking, being a recognisable relation) is only a sufficient, but not necessary, condition of decidability of path feasibility. This is because length assertions can easily deviate from recognisable relations (for instance
|x_{1}|=|x_{2}|
does not induce a recognisable relation over
\Sigma^{*}
), but decidability still remains.

0.6
Implementation

We have implemented our decision procedure for path feasibility in a
new tool OSTRICH, which is built on top of the SMT solver Princess
[
princess08
]
.
OSTRICH implements the optimised decision procedure for string functions as
described in Section
0.5.1
(i.e. using distributivity
of regular constraints across pre-images of functions)
and has built-in support for concatenation, reverse, FFT, and
\mathsf{replaceAll}
.
Moreover, since the optimisation only requires that string operations are functional, we can also support additional functions that satisfy
RegInvRel
, such as
{\sf replace}_{e}
which replaces only the first (leftmost and longest) match of a regular expression.
OSTRICH is extensible and new string functions can be added quite simply (Section
0.6.3
).

Our implementation adds a new theory solver for conjunctive formulas
representing path feasibility problems to Princess
(Section
0.6.1
).
This means that we support disjunction as well as conjunction in formulas,
as long as every conjunction of literals fed to the theory solver
corresponds to a path feasibility problem.
OSTRICH also implements a number of
optimisations to efficiently compute pre-images of relevant functions
(Section
0.6.2
). OSTRICH is entirely written in Scala and
is open-source.
We report on our experiments with OSTRICH in Section
.
The tool is available on GitHub
5
5

5

https://github.com/pruemmer/ostrich
.
The artifact is available on the ACM DL.

0.6.1
Depth-First Path Feasibility

Input:

Sets
\mathit{active},\mathit{passive}
of regex constraints

x\in L
;
acyclic set
\mathit{funApps}
of assignments
x:=f(\bar{y})
.

Result:

Either
\mathit{Model}(m)
with
m
a model satisfying
all constraints and function applications;

or
\mathit{Conflict}(s)
with
s
a set of regex constraints that
is inconsistent with
\mathit{funApps}
.

1

begin

2

if

\mathit{active}=\emptyset

then

3

/*
Extract a model by solving constraints and evaluating functions */

\mathit{leafTerms}\leftarrow\{x\mid x\text{~occurs in~}\mathit{passive}\cup\mathit{funApps}\}\setminus\{x\mid(x:=f(\bar{y}))\in\mathit{funApps}\}
;

4

\mathit{leafModel}\leftarrow\{x\mapsto w\mid x\in\mathit{leafTerms},~w\text{~satisfies all constraints on~}x\text{~in~}\mathit{passive}\}
;

5

m\leftarrow\mu m\,.\,\mathit{leafModel}\cup\{x\mapsto f(\bar{w})\mid(x:=f(\bar{y}))\in\mathit{funApps},~m(\bar{y})=\bar{w}\text{~is defined}\}
;

6

return

\mathit{Model}(m)

else

7

/*
Compute the pre-image of one of the active constraints */

choose a constraint
x\in L
in
\mathit{active}
;

8

if

there is an assignment
x:=f(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})
defining
x

in
\mathit{funApps}

then

9

\mathit{cset}\leftarrow\{x\in L\}
;

/*
start constructing a conflict set */

compute the pre-image
f^{-1}(L)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}L_{1}^{(i)}\times\cdots\times L_{r}^{(i)}
;

10

\mathit{act}\leftarrow\mathit{active}\setminus\{x\in L\},~~\mathit{pas}\leftarrow\mathit{passive}\cup\{x\in L\}
;

11

for

i\leftarrow 1

to

n

do

12

\mathit{newRegexes}\leftarrow\{y_{1}\in L_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,y_{r}\in L_{r}^{(i)}\}\setminus(\mathit{act}\cup\mathit{pas})
;

13

if

\mathit{act}\cup\mathit{pas}\cup\mathit{newRegexes}
is inconsistent

then

14

compute an unsatisfiable core
c\subseteq\mathit{act}\cup\mathit{pas}\cup\mathit{newRegexes}
;

15

\mathit{cset}\leftarrow\mathit{cset}\cup(c\setminus\{y_{1}\in L_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,y_{r}\in L_{r}^{(i)}\})
;

16

else

17

switch

\mathit{findModel}(\mathit{act}\cup\mathit{newRegexes},\;\mathit{pas},\;\mathit{funApps})

do

18

case

\mathit{Model}(m)

do

19

return

\mathit{Model}(m)
;

20

case

\mathit{Conflict}(s)

do

21

if

s\cap\mathit{newRegexes}=\emptyset

then

22

return

\mathit{Conflict}(s)
;

/*
back-jump */

else

23

\mathit{cset}\leftarrow\mathit{cset}\cup(s\setminus\{y_{1}\in L_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,y_{r}\in L_{r}^{(i)}\})
;

24

return

\mathit{Conflict}(cset)
;

/*
backtrack */

else

25

return

\mathit{findModel}(\mathit{active}\setminus\{x\in L\},\;\mathit{passive}\cup\{x\in L\},\;\mathit{funApps})
;

26

27

Algorithm 1

Recursive function
\mathit{findModel}

defining depth-first model construction for
SL

We first discuss the overall decision procedure for path feasibility
implemented in OSTRICH. The procedure performs depth-first exploration
of the search tree resulting from repeatedly splitting the
disjunctions (or unions) in the recognisable pre-images of
functions. Similar to the DPLL/CDCL architecture used in SAT solvers,
our procedure computes conflict sets and applies
back-jumping
[
tinelli_dpll_2004
]
to skip irrelevant parts
of the search tree.

For solving, a path feasibility problem is represented as a
set
\mathit{funApps}
of assignments
x:=f(\bar{y})
, and a set

\mathit{regex}
of regular expression constraints
x\in L
, with
x

being a string variable and
\bar{y}
a vector of string variables. The
set
\mathit{funApps}
by definition contains at most one assignment
for each variable
x
, and by nature of a path there are no
cyclic dependencies. We make two further simplifying assumptions:
(i) the set
\mathit{regex}
is on its own satisfiable, i.e., the
constraints given for each variable
x
are consistent; and (ii) for
each variable that occurs as the left-hand side of an
assignment
x:=f(\bar{y})
, there is at least one
constraint
x\in L
in the set
\mathit{regex}
. Assumption (i)
boils down to checking the non-emptiness of intersections of regular
languages, i.e., to a reachability check in the
product transition system, and can be done efficiently in practical
cases. Both assumptions could easily be relaxed, at the cost of making the
algorithm slightly more involved.

The exploration function

The algorithm is presented as a recursive
function
\mathit{findModel}
in pseudo-code in
Algorithm
1
. The function maintains two sets

\mathit{active},\mathit{passive}
of regular expression constraints,
kept as parameters, and in addition receives the set
\mathit{funApps}

as parameter.
Active
regular expression constraints are those
for which no pre-images have been computed yet, while

\mathit{passive}
are the constraints that have already gone through
pre-image computation. In the initial
call
\mathit{findModel}(\mathit{regex},\emptyset,\mathit{funApps})

of the function,
\mathit{active}
is chosen to be
\mathit{regex}
,
while
\mathit{passive}
is empty.

Depending on the status of a path feasibility problem,

\mathit{findModel}
can produce two outcomes:

•

\mathit{Model}(m)
, where
m
is maps string
variables to words that satisfy the regular expression
constraints in
\mathit{active}\cup\mathit{passive}
and the
assignments in
\mathit{funApps}
, interpreted as equations.

•

\mathit{Conflict}(s)
, with

s\subseteq\mathit{active}\cup\mathit{passive}
being a

conflict set
, i.e., a set of constraints that is inconsistent
with
\mathit{funApps}
. The set
s\cup\mathit{funApps}
can be
interpreted as an unsatisfiable core of the path feasibility
problem, and is used to identify irrelevant splits during the
search.

Implementation in detail

Model construction terminates with a positive result when the
set
\mathit{active}
becomes empty (line 2), in which case it is only
necessary to compute a model
m
(lines 3–6). For this, the algorithm
first computes the set of variables that do not occur on the left-hand
side of any assignment (line 3). The values of such leaf terms can be
chosen arbitrarily, as long as all derived regular expression
constraints are satisfied (line 4). The values of all other variables
are extracted from the assignments in
\mathit{funApps}
:
whenever an assignment
x:=f(\bar{y})
is found for which all
argument variables already have a value, also the value of the
left-hand side
x
is known (line 5).

Otherwise, one of the active constraints
x\in L
is selected for
pre-image computation (line 8). For the correctness it is irrelevant
in which order the constraints are selected, and branching heuristics
from the SAT world might be applicable. Our current implementation
selects the constraints in the fixed order in which the constrained
variables occur on the path, starting with constraints at the end of
the path. If
x
is a left-hand side of an assignment, the pre-image
of
L
is a recognisable relation that can be represented through
regular languages (line 11); the constraint
x\in L
then becomes
passive in subsequent recursive calls (line 12).

The algorithm then iterates over the disjuncts of the pre-image
(line 13), generates new regular expression constraints for the
function arguments (line 14), and checks whether any disjuncts
lead to a solution. During the iteration over disjuncts, the
algorithm builds up a conflict set
\mathit{cset}
collecting
constraints responsible for absence of a solution (lines 10,
17, 26). If the new constraints are inconsistent with
existing constraints (line 15), the disjunct does not have to be
considered further; the algorithm then computes a (possibly minimal)
unsatisfiable subset of the constraints, and adds it to the conflict
set
\mathit{cset}
. Otherwise,
\mathit{findModel}
is called
recursively (line 19). If the recursive call produces a model, no
further search is necessary, and the function returns
(lines 20-21). Similarly, if the recursive call reports a conflict
that is independent of the generated regular expression constraints,
it follows that no solution can exist for any of the disjuncts of the
pre-image, and the function can return immediately (lines 23–24). In
case of other conflicts, the conflict set
\mathit{cset}
is extended
(line 26), and finally returned to explain why no model could be found
(line 27).

0.6.2
Optimisation of Pre-Image Computation

We have optimised the pre-image computation of the concatenation and
\mathsf{replaceAll}
operations.

Concatenation

The pre-image of a regular language
L
for
concatenation
x:=y\circ z
can be computed by representing
L
as
an FA, say with
n
states, and generating a
union
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}L_{1}^{(i)}\times L_{2}^{(i)}
in which the
accepting state of
L_{1}^{(i)}
and the initial state of
L_{2}^{(i)}

iterate over all
n
states of
L

[
Abdulla14
]
. In practice, most of the resulting
n
cases are
immediately inconsistent with other regular expression constraints in

\mathit{active}\cup\mathit{passive}
; this happens for instance
when the length of
y
or
z
are already predetermined by other
constraints. Our implementation therefore filters the considered
languages
L_{1}^{(i)},L_{2}^{(i)}
upfront using length information
extracted from other constraints.

The
\mathsf{replaceAll}
Function

We implement
\mathsf{replaceAll}_{e}
by reduction to
\mathsf{replaceAll}_{a}
for a single character
a
.
We translate all

x:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{e}(y,z)

into

y^{\prime}:=T_{e}(y);x:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{\flat}(y^{\prime},z)

where
\flat
is a special character disjoint from the rest of the alphabet.
The transducer
T_{e}
copies the contents of
y
and replaces all left-most and longest subwords satisfying the regular expression
e
with
\flat
. This construction uses a
parsing automaton
and details can be found in
[
CCHLW18
]
.
We first recall how
\mathsf{replaceAll}_{a}
can be tackled, before explaining the inefficiencies and the solution we use.

Naive Recognisability

Suppose we have an FA
\mathcal{A}_{x}
giving a regular constraint on
x
.
We need to translate this automaton into a sequence of regular constraints on
y
and
z
.
To do this, we observe that all satisfying assignments to
x
must take the form

u_{1}w_{z}u_{2}w_{z}\ldots w_{z}u_{n}

where
w_{z}
is the value of
z
, and

u_{1}au_{2}a\ldots au_{n}

is the value of
y
.
Moreover, each word
u_{i}
cannot contain the character
a
since it would have been replaced by
w_{z}
.
The (satisfying) assignment to
x
must be accepted by
\mathcal{A}_{x}
.
Thus, we can extract from an accepting run of
\mathcal{A}_{x}
a set of pairs of states
Q_{z}
, which is the set of all pairs
(q,q^{\prime})
such that the run of
\mathcal{A}_{x}
moves from
q
to
q^{\prime}
while reading a copy of
w_{z}
.
Then, we can obtain a new FA
\mathcal{A}_{y}
by removing all
a
-transitions from
\mathcal{A}_{x}
and then adding
a
-transitions

(q,a,q^{\prime})

for each

(q,q^{\prime})\in Q_{z}
.
It is easy to verify that there is an accepting run of
\mathcal{A}_{y}
over

u_{1}au_{2}a\ldots au_{n}
.
Similarly, we define
\mathcal{A}_{z}
to be the intersection of
\mathcal{A}_{x}(q,\{q^{\prime}\})
for all
(q,q^{\prime})\in Q_{z}
.
We know by design that there is an accepting run of
\mathcal{A}_{z}
over
w_{z}
.

The value of
Q_{z}
above was extracted from an accepting run of
\mathcal{A}_{x}
.
There are, of course, many possible accepting runs of
\mathcal{A}_{x}
, each leading to a different value of
Q_{z}
, and thus a different
\mathcal{A}_{y}
and
\mathcal{A}_{z}
.
Since each
Q_{z}
a set of pairs of states of
\mathcal{A}_{x}
, there are only a finite number of values that can be taken by
Q_{z}
.
Thus, we can show the pre-image of

x:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{a}(y,a,z)

is recognisable by enumerating all possible values of
Q_{z}
.
For each
Q_{z}
we can produce a pair of automata

(\mathcal{A}^{Q_{z}}_{y},\mathcal{A}^{Q_{z}}_{z})

as described above.
Thus, the pre-image can be expressed by

\bigcup_{Q_{z}}\left(\mathcal{L}\mathord{\left(\mathcal{A}^{Q_{z}}_{x}\right)}\times\mathcal{L}\mathord{\left(\mathcal{A}^{Q_{z}}_{y}\right)}\right)
.

Optimised Recognisability

A problem with the above algorithm is that there are an exponential number of possible values of
Q_{z}
.
For example, if
\mathcal{A}_{x}
has
10
states, there are
2^{100}
possible values of
Q_{z}
;
it is infeasible to enumerate them all.
To reduce the number of considered pairs, we use the notion of a
Cayley Graph

[
Z81
,
D67
]
.
Note, this technique was already used by Chen
[
ChenThesis18
,
yan-tool
]
as part of an implementation of
[
CCHLW18
]
.

Given an automaton
\mathcal{A}
and a word
w
, we define

\llparenthesis w\rrparenthesis=\left\{{(q_{0},q_{n})}\ \middle|\ {\text{there exists a run $q_{0},\ldots,q_{n}$ of $\mathcal{A}$ over $w$}}\right\}.

The number of distinct

\llparenthesis w\rrparenthesis

is finite for a given FA.
We define Cayley Graphs in the context of FA.

Definition 0.6.1

(Cayley Graph)
.

Given an FA

\mathcal{A}=(\Sigma,Q,q_{0},F,\delta)

the
Cayley Graph
of
\mathcal{A}
is a graph

(V,E)

with nodes

V=\left\{{\llparenthesis w\rrparenthesis}\ \middle|\ {w\in\Sigma^{\ast}}\right\}

and edges

E=\left\{{(\llparenthesis w\rrparenthesis,a,\llparenthesis wa\rrparenthesis)}\ \middle|\ {w\in\Sigma^{\ast}\land a\in\Sigma}\right\}
.

For a given automaton, it is straight-forward to compute the Cayley Graph using a fixed point iteration: begin with
\llparenthesis\epsilon\rrparenthesis\in V
, then, until a fixed point is reached, take some
\llparenthesis w\rrparenthesis
in
V
and add
\llparenthesis wa\rrparenthesis
for all
a\in\Sigma
.
Note
\llparenthesis wa\rrparenthesis
is a simple composition of
\llparenthesis w\rrparenthesis
and

\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis=\left\{{(q,q^{\prime})}\ \middle|\ {(q,a,q^{\prime})\text{ is an edge of $\mathcal{A}$}}\right\}
.

We claim that instead of enumerating all
Q_{z}
, one only needs to consider all

\llparenthesis w\rrparenthesis\in V
.
Since
\llparenthesis w\rrparenthesis
can be a value of
Q_{z}
, the restriction does not increase the set of potential solutions.
We need to argue that it does not reduce them.
Hence, take some satisfying value

u_{1}w_{z}u_{2}w_{z}\ldots w_{z}u_{n}

of
x
.
One can easily verify that
w_{z}
is accepted by the
\mathcal{A}_{z}
constructed from
\llparenthesis w_{z}\rrparenthesis
.
Moreover,

u_{1}au_{2}a\ldots au_{n}

is accepted by the corresponding
\mathcal{A}_{y}
.
Thus we have not restricted the algorithm.

From experience, it is reasonable to hope that the Cayley Graph has far fewer nodes than the set of all potential
Q_{z}
.
Moreover, we can further improve the enumeration by considering any other regular constraints

\mathcal{A}^{1}_{z},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{m}_{z}

that may exist on the value of
z
.
As a simple example, if we had

\text{{assert}($z\in b^{\ast}$)};x:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{a}(y,z);\text{{assert}($x\in\mathcal{A}_{x}$)}

where
\mathcal{A}_{x}
has initial state
q_{0}
and accepting states
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
with transitions

(q_{0},a,q_{1})
and
(q_{0},b,q_{1})
, there is no need to consider

\llparenthesis a\rrparenthesis=\{(q_{0},q_{1})\}

since
z
cannot take the value
a
without violating

assert
(
z\in b^{\ast}
)
.

Using this observation, assume we already know that
z
’s value must be accepted by

\mathcal{A}^{1}_{z},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{m}_{z}
.
Instead of building the Cayley Graph alone, we build a product of the Cayley Graph and

\mathcal{A}^{1}_{z},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{m}_{z}

on the fly.
This product has states

(\llparenthesis w\rrparenthesis,q_{1},\ldots,q_{m})

for some
w\in\Sigma^{\ast}
and

q_{1}
, …,
q_{m}

states of

\mathcal{A}^{1}_{z},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{m}_{z}

respectively.
The only
\llparenthesis w\rrparenthesis
we need to consider are those such that

(\llparenthesis w\rrparenthesis,q_{1},\ldots,q_{m})

is a (reachable) product state, and, moreover,
q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}
are accepting states of

\mathcal{A}^{1}_{z},\ldots,\mathcal{A}^{m}_{z}
.

This technique first speeds up the construction of the Cayley Graph by limiting which nodes are generated, and second, avoids values of
Q_{z}
which are guaranteed to be unsatisfying.

0.6.3
Extensibility

One may extend OSTRICH to include any
string function with a recognisable pre-image
without having to worry
about other parts of the solver
. We give an example of adding a new

\mathsf{reverse}
function.

We have defined a Scala trait
PreOp}.
To add a string function, one defines a new Scala object with this trait.
This requires two methods described below.
An example object is given in Figure~\ref
fig:preop-reverse.

The first method is
eval}, which implements the string
function.
It takes a sequence of strings represented as sequence of integers.
For $\reverse$, this method reverses the argument.
For $\replaceall_a(x, y)$, the \mintinline
scalaeval function would take a sequence of two arguments (the values of
x
and
y
respectively) and replace all
a
characters in
x
with the value of
y
to produce the result.
The return value can be
None} if the function is not applicable to the given arguments.
The second method \mintinline
scalaapply performs the pre-image computation.
It takes two arguments: a sequence of constraints on the arguments (
argumentConstraints}), and a constraint on the result (\mintinline
scalaresultConstraint).
The result constraint is represented as an
Automaton} that accepts the language for which we are computing $f^
-1
.Theargumentconstraintsarerepresentedassequencesofsequencesofautomata:foreachargumentofthefunctiontherewillbeonesequenceofautomata.Theseconstraintsgivefurtherinformationonwhatisknownabouttheconstraintsontheargumentsofthefunction.Forexample,ifwearecomputing
x :=
reverse
(y)
andelsewherewehavedetermined
y
mustbeacceptedbyboth
\mathcal{A}
_1
and
\mathcal{A}
_2
,thenthefirst(andonly)elementoftheargumentconstraintswillbethesequence
\mathcal{A}
_1,
\mathcal{A}
_2
.Itisnotnecessarytousetheseconstraints,buttheymayhelptooptimisethepre-imagecomputation(asinthecaseof
replaceAll
{{describedabove).\par Thereturnvalueof\verb{apply} is a pair.
The first element is the pre-image (a recognisable relation).

of the relation.
The second element is a list of the argument constraints used during the pre-image computation; this information is needed to compute correct

\par To complete the addition of the function, the reverse function is
registered in the OSTRICH string theory object. The function is then
ready to be used in an SMT-LIB problem, e.g. by writing the assertion
\verb+(assert (= x (user_reverse y)))+.
\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Experiments}
\par We have compared OSTRICH with a number of existing solvers on a wide range of benchmarks.
In particular, we compared OSTRICH with
CVC4~1.6~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{cvc4}{}{}]},
SLOTH~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{HJLRV18}{}{}]}, and
Z3
configured to use the Z3-str3 string solver~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Z3-str3}{}{}]}.
We considered several sets of benchmarks which are described in the next sub-section.
The results are given in Section~\ref{sec:results}.
\par In~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{HJLRV18}{}{}]} SLOTH was compared with S3P~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{TCJ16}{}{}]} where inconsistent behaviour was reported.
We contacted the S3P authors who report that the current code is unsupported;
moreover, S3P is being integrated with Z3.
Hence, we do not compare with this tool.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Benchmarks}
\par The first set of benchmarks we call {Transducer}.
It combines the benchmark sets of Stranger \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Stranger}{}{}]} and the mutation XSS benchmarks of~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LB16}{}{}]}.
The first (sub-)set appeared in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{HJLRV18}{}{}]} and contains instances manually derived from PHP programs taken from the website of Stranger~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Stranger}{}{}]}.
It contains 10 formulae (5 sat, 5 unsat) each testing for the absence of the vulnerability pattern \verb+.*<script.*+ in the program output.
These formulae contain between 7 and 42 variables, with an average of 21.
The number of atomic constraints ranges between 7 and 38 and averages 18.
These examples use disjunction, conjunction, regular constraints, and
concatenation, $\mathsf{replaceAll}$. They also contain several one-way functional
transducers (defined in SMTLIB in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{HJLRV18}{}{}]}) encoding functions such as
\verb+addslashes+ and \verb+trim+ used by the programs. Note that transducers
have been known for some time to be a good framework for
specifying sanitisers and browser transductions
(e.g., see the works by Minamide, Veanes, Saxena, and others
\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Min05,BEK,web-model,DV13}{}{}]}), and a library of transducer specifications for
such functions is available (e.g.~see the language BEK \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{BEK}{}{}]}).
\par The second (sub-)set was used by~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LB16,HJLRV18}{}{}]} and consists of 8
formulae taken from~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LB16,Kern14}{}{}]}.
These examples explore mutation XSS vulnerabilities in JavaScript programs.
They contain between 9 and 12 variables, averaging 9.75, and 9-13 atomic constraints, with an average of 10.5.
They use conjunctions, regular constraints, concatenation, $\mathsf{replaceAll}$, and transducers providing functions such as \verb+htmlEscape+ and \verb+escapeString+.
\par Our next set of benchmarks, {SLOG},
came from the SLOG tool~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{fang-yu-circuits}{}{}]} and consist of 3,392 instances.
They are derived from the security analysis of real web applications and contain 1-211 string variables (average 6.5) and 1-182 atomic formula (average 5.8).
We split these benchmarks into two sets {SLOG (replace)}\ and {SLOG (replaceall)}.
Each use conjunction, disjunction, regular constraints, and concatenation.
The set {SLOG (replace)}\ contains 3,391 instances and uses ${\sf replace}$.
{SLOG (replaceall)}\ contains 120 instances using the $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ operation.
\par Our next set of benchmarks {Kaluza}\ is the well-known set of \emph{Kaluza}
benchmarks~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Berkeley-JavaScript}{}{}]} restricted to those instances which
satisfy our semantic conditions (roughly ${\sim}80$\% of the benchmarks).
Kaluza contains concatenation, regular
constraints, and length constraints, most of which admit regular monadic
decomposition.
There are 37,090 such benchmarks (28\,032 sat).
\par We also considered the benchmark set of~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{yan-tool,ChenThesis18}{}{}]}.
This contains 42 hand-crafted benchmarks using regular constraints, concatenation, and $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ with variables in both argument positions.
The benchmarks contain 3-7 string variables and 3-9 atomic constraints.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Results}
\par We compared the tools on an AMD Opteron 2220 SE machine,
running 64-bit Linux and Java~1.8, with the heap space of
each job limited to 2~GB.
We used a timeout of 240s for each Kaluza problem, and 600s for
the other benchmarks.
Figure~\ref{fig:results} summarises our findings as cactus plots.
For each benchmark set, we plot the time in seconds on a cubic-root scale against the number of benchmarks solved (individually) within that time.
The extent of each line on the Time axis gives the maximum time in seconds required to solve any instance in the set.
When a solver is not plotted it is because it was unable to analyse the benchmark set.
\par\begin{figure}\includegraphics[width]{cactus-plots}
\vskip-17.22217pt\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{4}}{Comparison of solvers on several sets of benchmarks.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 4}}{\small Comparison of solvers on several sets of benchmarks.}}}\end{figure}
\par For the {Transducer}\ set, OSTRICH solved all benchmarks taking a maximum of 4s.
SLOTH did not answer 2 instances and was slower on the rest.
This set is not supported by CVC4 or Z3-str3.
\par For the {SLOG (replaceall)}\ set, OSTRICH solved all 120 instances within a few seconds, while SLOTH only solved 100.
CVC4 and Z3-str3 were omitted as they do not support $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ constraints.
\par For the {SLOG (replace)}\ set, OSTRICH was also able to solve all instances within the timeout.
CVC4 was able to solve all but 13 of the benchmarks.
Similarly, SLOTH was unable to solve 138 instances, while Z3-str3 could not solve 1,118.
We note that Z3-str3 gave inconsistent results for 18 of these benchmarks,
an issue that could not be conclusively resolved before submission..
\par For the {Kaluza}\ set, CVC4, Z3-str3, and OSTRICH were able to solve all instances.
Since SLOTH does not support length constraints, it reported errors in 81 of these benchmarks.
Otherwise all instances were solved within the timeout.
\par We were unable to install the tool of~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{yan-tool}{}{}]} for comparison with the benchmarks of~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{yan-tool,ChenThesis18}{}{}]}.
Since OSTRICH was the only available tool supporting variables in both arguments of $\mathsf{replaceAll}$, we do not provide a plot.
We note that the most difficult benchmark took OSTRICH 1.56s to answer, with the second hardest requiring 0.34s.
\par Overall, CVC4 was the fastest for the constraints it was able to answer,
while
OSTRICH was the only solver which answered all benchmark instances.
However, the runtime differences are fractions of a second.
In terms of completeness guarantees and the type of string constraints supported, SLOTH is our nearest competitor, with the main difference being that OSTRICH supports variables in both argument positions of $\mathsf{replaceAll}$, while SLOTH will only accept constant strings as the second argument.
Our results show that OSTRICH outperforms SLOTH on all benchmark sets.
\par
\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusion}
\par We proposed two general semantic conditions which together ensure the
decidability of path feasibility with complex string operations including
$\mathsf{replaceAll}$, transducers, and concatenation. Our semantic conditions are
satisfied by a
wide range of complex string operations and subsume various existing string
constraint languages
(e.g.~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LB16,CCHLW18}{}{}]}) and many current existing benchmarks
(e.g.~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Berkeley-JavaScript,HJLRV18,Stranger,fang-yu-circuits,LB16}{}{}]}).
Based on the semantic conditions, we developed a conceptually simple and
generic decision procedure with an extensible architecture that allow a user to
easily incorporate a user-defined function.
After providing theoretical evidence via computational complexity that these
semantic conditions might be too general to provide an efficient decision
procedure, we proposed two different solutions.
We advocated the first solution (prohibit nondeterminism in string operations)
whenever possible since it permits a highly effective optimisation of the solver
based on a kind of distributivity property of regular constraints across string
functions. In fact, the extra restriction imposed by this is satisfied by
most (but not all) existing benchmarking examples.
We developed a new string solver OSTRICH that implements the first solution and
demonstrate its
efficacy against other competitive solvers on most existing benchmarks.

\par\par\lx@newpage\par\par\begin{center}{\huge Appendix} \\
{\large`` Decision Procedures for Path Feasibility of String-Manipulating
Programs with Complex Operations''}
\end{center}
\par\vskip 12.0pt plus 4.0pt minus 4.0pt\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:nexpspace-hardness}: Hardness of $\strline$ with 2FTs+conc and $\strline$ with FTs+$\replaceall$}
\par We first give a full definition of tiling problems before showing the result.
We give the details here of the proof for $\mathsf{SL}$ with 2FTS+conc.
The proof can easily be adapted to the case of FTs~$\mathsf{replaceAll}$ using the simulation described in Section~\ref{sec:two-way-simulation}.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Tiling Problems}
\par A \emph{tiling problem} is a tuple
$\left(\Theta,H,V,t_{I},t_{F}\right)$
where
$\Theta$ is a finite set of tiles,
$H\subseteq\Theta\times\Theta$ is a horizontal matching relation,
$V\subseteq\Theta\times\Theta$ is a vertical matching relation, and
$t_{I},t_{F}\in\Theta$ are initial and final tiles respectively.
\par A solution to a tiling problem over a $m$-width corridor is a sequence
$$\begin{array}[]{c}t^{1}_{1}\ldots t^{1}_{m}\\
t^{2}_{1}\ldots t^{2}_{m}\\
\ldots\\
t^{h}_{1}\ldots t^{h}_{m}\end{array}$$
where
$t^{1}_{1}=t_{I}$,
$t^{h}_{m}=t_{F}$,
and for all
$1\leq i<m$
and
$1\leq j\leq h$
we have
$\left(t^{j}_{i},t^{j}_{i+1}\right)\in H$
and for all
$1\leq i\leq m$
and
$1\leq j<h$
we have
$\left(t^{j}_{i},t^{j+1}_{i}\right)\in V$.
Note, we will assume that $t_{I}$ and $t_{F}$ can only appear at the beginning and end of the tiling respectively.
\par Tiling problems characterise many complexity classes~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{BGG97}{}{}]}.
In particular, we will use the following facts.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@For any $m$-space Turing machine, there exists a tiling problem of size polynomial in the size of the Turing machine, over a corridor of width $m$, that has a solution iff the $m$-space Turing machine has a terminating computation.
In particular we will consider problems where the corridor width is
$2^{:^{2^{m}}}$
where the height of the stack of exponentials is $n$.
E.g.\ when $n$ is $0$ the width is $m$, when $n$ is $1$ the width is $2^{m}$, when $n$ is $2$ the width is $2^{2^{m}}$ and so on.
Solving tiling problems of width
$2^{:^{2^{m}}}$
is complete for the same amount of space.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@There is a fixed
$\left(\Theta,H,V,t_{I},t_{F}\right)$
such that for any width $m$ there is a unique solution
$$\begin{array}[]{c}t^{1}_{1}\ldots t^{1}_{m}\\
t^{2}_{1}\ldots t^{2}_{m}\\
\ldots\\
t^{h}_{1}\ldots t^{h}_{m}\end{array}$$and moreover $h$ is exponential in $m$.
One such example is a Turing machine where the tape contents represent a binary number.
The Turing machine starts from a tape containing only $0$s and finishes with a tape containing only $1$s by repeatedly incrementing the binary encoding on the tape.
This Turing machine can be encoded as the required tiling problem.
\end{itemize}
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Large Numbers}
\par The crux of the proof is encoding large numbers that can take values between $1$ and $n$-fold exponential.
\par A linear-length binary number could be encoded simply as a sequence of bits
$$b_{0}\ldots b_{m}\in\{0,1\}^{m}\ .$$
To aid with later constructions we will take a more oblique approach.
Let
$\left(\Theta_{1},H_{1},V_{1},t_{I}^{1},t_{F}^{1}\right)$
be a copy of the fixed tiling problem from the previous section for which there is a unique solution, whose length must be exponential in the width.
In the future, we will need several copies of this problem, hence the indexing here.
Fix a width $m$ and let $\text{MAX}_{1}$ be the corresponding corridor length.
A \emph{level-1} number can encode values from $1$ to $\text{MAX}_{1}$.
In particular, for $1\leq i\leq\text{MAX}_{1}$ we define
$$[i]_{1}=t^{i}_{1}\ldots t^{i}_{m}$$
where
$t^{i}_{1}\ldots t^{i}_{m}$
is the tiling of the $i$th row of the unique solution to the tiling problem.
\par A \emph{level-2} number will be derived from tiling a corridor of width $\text{MAX}_{1}$, and thus the number of rows will be doubly-exponential.
For this, we require another copy
$\left(\Theta_{2},H_{2},V_{2},t_{I}^{2},t_{F}^{2}\right)$
of the above tiling problem.
Moreover, let $\text{MAX}_{2}$ be the length of the solution for a corridor of width $\text{MAX}_{1}$.
Then for any
$1\leq i\leq\text{MAX}_{2}$
we define
$$[i]_{2}=[1]_{1}t^{i}_{1}[2]_{1}t^{i}_{2}\ldots[\text{MAX}_{1}]_{1}t^{i}_{\text{MAX}_{1}}$$
where
$t^{i}_{1}\ldots t^{i}_{\text{MAX}_{1}}$
is the tiling of the $i$th row of the unique solution to the tiling problem.
That is, the encoding indexes each tile with it's column number, where the column number is represented as a level-1 number.
\par In general, a \emph{level-$n$} number is of length $(n-1)$-fold exponential and can encode numbers $n$-fold exponential in size.
We use a copy
$\left(\Theta_{n},H_{n},V_{n},t_{I}^{n},t_{F}^{n}\right)$
of the above tiling problem and use a corridor of width
$\text{MAX}_{n-1}$.
We define $\text{MAX}_{n}$ as the length of the unique solution to this problem.
Then, for any $1\leq i\leq\text{MAX}_{n}$ we have
$$[i]_{n}=[1]_{(n-1)}t^{i}_{1}[2]_{(n-1)}t^{i}_{2}\ldots[\text{MAX}_{(n-1)}]_{(n-1)}t^{i}_{\text{MAX}_{(n-1)}}$$
where
$t^{i}_{1}\ldots t^{i}_{\text{MAX}_{n-1}}$
is the tiling of the $i$th row of the unique solution to the tiling problem.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Recognising Large Numbers}
\par We first define a useful program
${S_{n}}\mathord{\left(x\right)}$
with a single input string $x$ which can only be satisfied if $x$ is of the following form, where
$\bowtie$, $\oplus$, and $\#$
are auxiliary symbols.
$$\begin{array}[]{c}{S_{n}}\mathord{\left(x\right)}\text{ is satisfiable}\\
\iff\\
x\in\left(\left([1]_{n}\left(\bowtie[1]_{n}\right)^{\ast}\right)\oplus\left([2]_{n}\left(\bowtie[2]_{n}\right)^{\ast}\right)\oplus\ldots\oplus\left([\text{MAX}_{n}]_{n}\left(\bowtie[\text{MAX}_{n}]_{n}\right)^{\ast}\right)\#\right)^{\ast}\end{array}$$
That is, the string must contain sequences of strings that count from $1$ to $\text{MAX}_{n}$.
This counting may stutter and repeat a number several times before moving to the next.
A separator $\bowtie$ indicates a stutter, while $\oplus$ indicates that the next number must add one to the current number.
Finally, a $\#$ ends a sequence and may start again from $[1]_{n}$.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Base case $\expheight= 1$.}
\par We define
$${S_{1}}\mathord{\left(x\right)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}y:={T}\mathord{\left(x\right)};\\
\textbf{assert}(y\in\top)\end{array}\right.$$
where $\top$ is a character output by $T$ when $x$ is correctly encoded.
Otherwise $T$ outputs $\bot$.
\par We describe how $T$ operates.
Because $T$ is two-way, it may perform several passes of the input $x$.
Recall that $T$ requires $x$ to contain a word of the form
$$\left(\left([1]_{1}\left(\bowtie[1]_{1}\right)^{\ast}\right)\oplus\left([2]_{1}\left(\bowtie[2]_{1}\right)^{\ast}\right)\oplus\ldots\oplus\left([\text{MAX}_{1}]_{1}\left(\bowtie[\text{MAX}_{1}]_{1}\right)^{\ast}\right)\#\right)^{\ast}$$
and each $[i]_{1}$ is the $i$th row of the unique solution to the tiling problem of width $m$.
The passes proceed as follows.
If a passes fails, the transducer outputs $\bot$ and terminates.
If all passes succeed, the transducer outputs $\top$ and terminates.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@During the first pass $T$ verifies that the input is of the form
$$\left(\Theta_{1}^{m}\left(\{\bowtie,\oplus\}\Theta_{1}^{m}\right)^{\ast}\#\right)^{\ast}\ .$$\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@During the second pass the transducer verifies that the first block of
$\Theta_{1}^{m}$,
and all blocks of
$\Theta_{1}^{m}$
immediately following a $\#$ have $t_{I}^{1}$ as the first tile.
Simultaneously, it can verify that all blocks of
$\Theta_{1}^{m}$
immediately preceding a $\#$ finish with the tile $t_{F}^{1}$.
Moreover, it checks that $t_{I}^{1}$ and $t_{F}^{1}$ do not appear elsewhere.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@During the third pass $T$ verifies the horizontal tiling relation.
That is, every contiguous pair of tiles $t,t^{\prime}$ in $x$ must be such that
$(t,t^{\prime})\in H_{1}$.
This can easily be done by storing the last character read into the states of $T$.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@The vertical tiling relation and equality checks are verified using $m$ more passes.
During the $j$th pass, the $j$th column is tested.
The transducer $T$ stores in its state the tile in the $j$th column of the first block of $\Theta_{1}^{m}$ or any block immediately following $\#$.
(The transducer can count to $m$ in its state.)
It then moves to the $j$th column of the next block of $\Theta_{1}^{m}$, remembering whether the blocks were separated with $\bowtie$, $\oplus$, or $\#$.
If the separator was $\bowtie$ the transducer checks that the $j$th tile of the current block matches the tile stored in the state (i.e.~is equal to the preceding block).
If the separator was $\oplus$ the transducer checks that the $j$th tile of the current block is related by $V_{1}$ to the stored tile.
In this case the current $j$th tile is stored and the previously stored tile forgotten.
Finally, if the separator was $\#$ there is nothing to check.
If any check fails, the pass will also fail.
\end{itemize}
\par If all passes succeed, we know that $x$ contains a word where blocks separated by $\bowtie$ are equal
(since all positions are equal, as verified individually by the final $m$ passes),
blocks separated by $\oplus$ satisfy $V_{1}$ in all positions,
the $t_{I}^{1}$ tile appears at the start of all sequences separated by $\#$ and each such sequence ends with $t_{F}^{1}$, and
finally the horizontal tiling relation is satisfied at all times.
Thus, $x$ must be of the form
$$\left(\left([1]_{1}\left(\bowtie[1]_{1}\right)^{\ast}\right)\oplus\left([2]_{1}\left(\bowtie[2]_{1}\right)^{\ast}\right)\oplus\ldots\oplus\left([\text{MAX}_{1}]_{1}\left(\bowtie[\text{MAX}_{1}]_{1}\right)^{\ast}\right)\#\right)^{\ast}$$
as required.
\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Inductive case $\expheight$.}
\par We define a program
${S_{n}}\mathord{\left(x\right)}$
such that
$$\begin{array}[]{c}{S_{n}}\mathord{\left(x\right)}\text{ is satisfiable}\\
\iff\\
x\in\left(\left([1]_{n}\left(\bowtie[1]_{n}\right)^{\ast}\right)\oplus\left([2]_{n}\left(\bowtie[2]_{n}\right)^{\ast}\right)\oplus\ldots\oplus\left([\text{MAX}_{n}]_{n}\left(\bowtie[\text{MAX}_{n}]_{n}\right)^{\ast}\right)\#\right)^{\ast}\ .\end{array}$$
Assume, by induction, we have a program
${S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(x\right)}$
which already satisfies this property (for $n-1$).
We define
$${S_{n}}\mathord{\left(x\right)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}y={T}\mathord{\left(x\right)};\\
{S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}\end{array}\right.$$
where $T$ is a transducer that behaves as described below.
Note, the reference to
${S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$
is not a procedure call, since these are not supported by our language.
Instead, the procedure is inlined, with its input variable $x$ replaced by $y$ and other variables renamed to avoid clashes.
\par The transducer will perform several passes to make several checks.
If a check fails it will halt and output a symbol $\bot$, which means that $y$ can no longer satisfy
${S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$.
During normal execution $T$ will make checks that rely on level-$(n-1)$ numbers appearing in the correct sequence or being equal.
To ensure these properties hold, $T$ will write these numbers to $y$ and rely on these properties then being verified by
${S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$.
The passes behave as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@During the first pass $T$ verifies that $x$ belongs to the regular language
$$\left(\left(\left(\left(\left(\Theta_{1}^{m}\Theta_{2}\right)^{\ast}\Theta_{3}\right)^{\ast}\cdots\right)^{\ast}\Theta_{n}\right)^{\ast}\left(\{\bowtie,\oplus\}\left(\left(\left(\left(\Theta_{1}^{m}\Theta_{2}\right)^{\ast}\Theta_{3}\right)^{\ast}\cdots\right)^{\ast}\Theta_{n}\right)^{\ast}\right)^{\ast}\#\right)^{\ast}\ .$$This can be done with a polynomial number of states.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@During the second pass $T$ will verify that the first instance of
$\Theta_{n}$
appearing in the word or after a $\#$ is $t_{I}^{n}$.
Similarly, the final instance of any
$\Theta_{n}$
before any $\#$ is $t_{F}^{n}$.
Moreover, it checks that
$t_{I}^{n}$
and
$t_{F}^{n}$
do not appear elsewhere.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@During the third pass $T$ will verify the horizontal tiling relation
$H_{n}$.
Each block (separated by $\bowtie$, $\oplus$, or $\#$) is checked in turn.
There are two components to this.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The indexing of the tiles must be correct.
That is, the first tile of the block must be indexed
$[1]_{n-1}$,
the second
$[2]_{n-1}$,
through to
$[\text{MAX}_{n-1}]_{n-1}$.
Thus, $T$ copies directly the instance of
$\left(\left(\left(\Theta_{1}^{m}\Theta_{2}\right)^{\ast}\Theta_{3}\right)^{\ast}\cdots\right)^{\ast}$
preceding each
$\Theta_{n}$
to the output tape, followed immediately by
$\oplus$
as long as the character after
$\Theta_{n}$
is not a separator from
$\{\bowtie,\oplus,\#\}$.
Otherwise, it is the end of the block and $\#$ is written.
\par Hence,
${S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$
will verify that the output for each block is
$[1]_{n-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus[\text{MAX}_{n-1}]_{n-1}$
which enforces that the indexing of the tiles is correct.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Horizontally adjacent tiles must satisfy
$H_{n}$.
This is done by simply storing the last read tile from
$\Theta_{n}$
in the state of $T$.
Then whenever a new tile from
$\Theta_{n}$
is seen without a separator $\bowtie$, $\oplus$, or $\#$, then it can be checked against the previous tile and
$H_{n}$.
\end{itemize}\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@The transducer $T$ then performs a non-deterministic number of passes to check the vertical tiling relation.
We will use
${S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$
to ensure that $T$ in fact performs
$\text{MAX}_{n-1}$
passes, the first checking the first column of the tiling over
$\Theta_{n}$,
the second checking the second column, and so on up to the
$\text{MAX}_{n-1}$th column.
\par Note, we know from the previous pass that each row of the tiling is indexed correctly.
In the sequel, let us use the term ``session'' to refer to the sequences of characters separated by $\#$.
\par Each pass of $T$ checks a single column (across all sessions).
At the start of each session, $T$ moves non-deterministically to the start of some block
$\left(\left(\left(\Theta_{1}^{m}\Theta_{2}\right)^{\ast}\Theta_{3}\right)^{\ast}\cdots\right)^{\ast}\Theta_{n}$
(without passing $\bowtie$, $\oplus$, or $\#$).
It then copies the tiles from
$\left(\left(\left(\Theta_{1}^{m}\Theta_{2}\right)^{\ast}\Theta_{3}\right)^{\ast}\cdots\right)^{\ast}$
to $y$ and saves the tile from
$\Theta_{n}$
in its state before moving to the next separator from
$\{\bowtie,\oplus,\#\}$.
In the case of $\#$ nothing needs to be checked and $T$ continues to the next session or finishes the pass if there are no more sessions.
In the case of $\bowtie$ or $\oplus$ the transducer remembers this separator and moves non-deterministically to the start of some block (without passing another $\bowtie$, $\oplus$, or $\#$).
It then writes $\bowtie$ to $y$ as it is intended that $T$ choose the same column as before.
This will be verified by
${S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$.
To aid with this $T$ copies the tiles from
$\left(\left(\left(\Theta_{1}^{m}\Theta_{2}\right)^{\ast}\Theta_{3}\right)^{\ast}\cdots\right)^{\ast}$
to $y$.
It can then check the tile from
$\Theta_{n}$.
If the remembered separator was $\bowtie$ then this tile must match the saved one.
If it was $\oplus$ then this tile must be related by
$V_{n}$
to the saved one.
If this succeeds , $T$ stores the new tile and forgets the old and continues to the next separator to continue checking
$V_{n}$.
\par At the end of the pass (checking a single column from all sessions) then $T$ will either have failed and written $\bot$ or written a sequence of level-$(n-1)$ numbers to $y$ separated by $\bowtie$. That is
$[i_{1}]_{n-1}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie[i_{\alpha}]_{n-1}$
for some $\alpha$.
Since, by induction,
${S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$
is correct, then the program can only be satisfied if $T$ chose the same position in each row.
That is
$i_{1}=\cdots=i_{\alpha}$.
Thus, the vertical relation for the $i_{1}$th column has been verified.
\par At this point $T$ can either write $\#$ and terminate or perform another pass (non-deterministically).
In the latter case, it outputs $\oplus$, moves back to the beginning of the tape, and starts again.
Thus, after a number of passes, $T$ will have written
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\hskip 42.67912pt$\left([i^{1}_{1}]_{n-1}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie[i^{1}_{\alpha_{1}}]_{n-1}\right)\oplus\cdots\oplus\left([i^{\beta}_{1}]_{n-1}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie[i^{\beta}_{\alpha_{\beta}}]_{n-1}\right)\#$
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\par for some $\beta$, $\alpha_{1}$, \ldots, $\alpha_{\beta}$.
Since
${S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$
will only accept such sequences of the form
$$\left([1]_{n-1}\left(\bowtie[1]_{n-1}\right)^{\ast}\right)\oplus\cdots\oplus\left([\text{MAX}_{n-1}]_{n-1}\left(\bowtie[\text{MAX}_{n-1}]_{n-1}\right)^{\ast}\right)\#$$we know that $T$ must check each vertical column in turn, from $1$ to
$\text{MAX}_{n-1}$.
\end{itemize}
\par Thus, at the end of all passes, if $T$ has not output $\bot$ it has verified that $x$ is a correct encoding of a solution to
$\left(\Theta_{n},H_{n},V_{n},t_{I}^{n},t_{F}^{n}\right)$.
That is, together with
${S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$
we know that
the word is of the correct format,
each row has a tile for each index and these indices appear in order,
the horizontal relation is respected, and
the vertical tiling relation is respected.
If $x$ is not a correct encoding then $T$ will not be able to produce a $y$ that satisfies
${S_{n-1}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$.
\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Reducing from a Tiling Problem}
\par Now that we are able to encode large numbers, we can encode an $n$-$\textsc{expspace}$-hard tiling problem as a satisfiability problem of $\mathsf{SL}[T]$ with two-way transducers.
In fact, most of the technical work has been done.
\par Thus, fix a tiling problem
$\left(\Theta,H,V,t_{I},t_{F}\right)$
that is $n$-$\textsc{expspace}$-hard.
In particular, we allow a corridor $\text{MAX}_{n}$ tiles wide.
We use the program
$$S=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}y={T}\mathord{\left(x\right)};\\
{S_{n}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}\end{array}\right.$$
where $T$ is defined exactly as in the inductive case of
${S_{n}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$
except the tiling problem used is
$\left(\Theta,H,V,t_{I},t_{F}\right)$
rather than
$\left(\Theta_{n},H_{n},V_{n},t_{I}^{n},t_{F}^{n}\right)$.
\par A satisfying tiling
$$\begin{array}[]{c}t^{1}_{1}\ldots t^{1}_{\text{MAX}_{n}}\\
\cdots\\
t^{h}_{1}\ldots t^{h}_{\text{MAX}_{n}}\end{array}$$
can be encoded
$$[1]_{n}t^{1}_{1}\ldots[\text{MAX}_{n}]_{n}t^{1}_{\text{MAX}_{n}}\oplus\cdots\oplus[1]_{n}t^{n}_{1}\ldots[\text{MAX}_{n}]_{n}t^{n}_{\text{MAX}_{n}}\#$$
which will satisfy $S$ in the same way as a correct input to
${S_{n}}\mathord{\left(y\right)}$.
To see this, note that
$$[1]_{n}t^{i}_{1}\ldots[\text{MAX}_{n}]_{n}t^{i}_{\text{MAX}_{n}}$$
acts like some
$[i]_{n+1}$.
\par In the opposite direction, assume some input satisfying $S$.
Arguing as in the encoding of large numbers, this input must be of the form
$$\left(\left(r_{1}\left(\bowtie r_{1}\right)^{\ast}\right)\oplus\left(r_{2}\left(\bowtie r_{2}\right)^{\ast}\right)\oplus\cdots\oplus\left(r_{h}\left(\bowtie r_{h}\right)^{\ast}\right)\#\right)^{\ast}$$
where each $r_{i}$ is a row of a correct solution to the tiling problem.
\par Thus, with $n+1$ transducers, we can encode a $n$-$\textsc{expspace}$-hard problem.
\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop-fft-upper}}
\par We start with a remark that, as mentioned the concatenation function $\circ$ can be encoded by the $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ function (cf. \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CCHLW18}{}{}]} for details), so we shall not discuss $\circ$ separately in the proof.
\par The following lemma is crucial for showing Proposition~\ref{prop-fft-upper}.
\begin{lemma}Let $(\mathcal{A},\Omega)$ be a conjunctive FA. Then for each string function $f$ in $\mathsf{SL}$[$\circ$, $\mathsf{replaceAll}$, $\mathsf{reverse}$, FFT]{},
there is an algorithm that runs in $(\ell_{f}(|f|,|(\mathcal{A},\Omega)|))^{c_{0}}$ space (where $c_{0}$ is a constant) which enumerates
each disjunct of a conjunctive representation of $\textsf{Pre}_{R_{f}}((\mathcal{A},\Omega))$, whose atom size is bounded by $\ell_{f}(|f|,|(\mathcal{A},\Omega)|)$, where
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@if $f$ is $\mathsf{replaceAll}_{e}$ for a regular expression $e$, then $|f|=|e|$ and $\ell_{f}(i,j)=2^{c_{1}i^{c_{2}}}j$ for some constants $c_{1},c_{2}$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@if $f$ is $\mathsf{reverse}$, then $|f|=1$ and $\ell_{f}(i,j)=j$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@if $f$ is defined by an FFT{} $T$, then $|f|=|T|$ and $\ell_{f}(i,j)=ij$.
\end{itemize}\end{lemma}
\par\par\par\par\par\@proof Let $\mathcal{A}=(Q,\delta),\Omega)$ be a conjunctive FA.
\par For $f=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{e}$, the result was shown in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CCHLW18}{}{}]} .
\par For the $\mathsf{reverse}$ function, $\textsf{Pre}_{R_{\mathsf{reverse}}}((\mathcal{A},\Omega))$ is exactly the language defined by $(\mathcal{A}^{\pi},\Omega^{\pi})$, where
$\Omega^{\pi}=\{(q_{2},q_{1})\mid(q_{1},q_{2})\in\Omega\}$.
\par If $f$ is defined by an FFT{} $T=(Q^{\prime},q^{\prime}_{0},F^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})$, then $\textsf{Pre}_{R_{T}}(\mathcal{A},\Omega)$ is conjunctively represented by $((\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\Omega_{q^{\prime}}))_{q^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}}$, where
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}=(Q^{\prime\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime})$, with $Q^{\prime\prime}=Q^{\prime}\times Q$, $\delta^{\prime\prime}$ comprises the transitions $((q^{\prime}_{1},q_{1}),a,(q^{\prime}_{2},q_{2}))$ such that there exists $w\in\Sigma^{*}$ satisfying that $(q^{\prime}_{1},a,w,q^{\prime}_{2})\in\delta^{\prime}$ and $q_{1}\xrightarrow[\mathcal{A}]{w}q_{2}$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\Omega_{q^{\prime}}=\{((q^{\prime}_{0},q_{1}),(q^{\prime},q_{2}))\mid(q_{1},q_{2})\in\Omega\}$.
\end{itemize}
Note that the language defined by $((\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\Omega_{q^{\prime}}))_{q^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}}$ is $\bigcup\limits_{q^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\Omega_{q^{\prime}})$. The size of each atom $(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\Omega_{q^{\prime}})$ is $|T||\mathcal{A}|=|f||\mathcal{A}|$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\@proof[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop-fft-upper}]
Let $S$ be a program in $\mathsf{SL}$[$\circ$, $\mathsf{replaceAll}$, $\mathsf{reverse}$, FFT]{}. For technical convenience, we consider the \emph{dependency graph} of $S$, denoted by $G_{S}=(V_{S},E_{S})$, where $V_{S}$ is the set of string variables in $S$, and $E_{S}$ comprises the edges $(y,x_{j})$ for each assignment $y:=f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})$ in $S$.
\par Recall that the decision procedure in the proof of Theorem~\ref{th:gen} works by repeatedly removing the last assignment, say $y:=f(\vec{x})$, and generating new assertions involving $\vec{x}$ from the assertions of $y$.
We adapt that decision procedure as follows:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@Replace FA{}s with conjunctive FA{}s and use the conjunctive representations of the pre-images of string operations.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Before removing the assignments, a preprocessing is carried out for $S$ as follows: For each assertion $\textbf{assert}(R(\vec{x}))$ with $\vec{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})$ in $S$, nondeterministically guess a disjunct of the conjunctive representation of $R$, say $((\mathcal{A}_{1},\Omega_{1}),\ldots,(\mathcal{A}_{r},\Omega_{r}))$, and replace $\textbf{assert}(R(\vec{x}))$ with the sequence of assertions $\textbf{assert}(x_{1}\in(\mathcal{A}_{1},\Omega_{1}));\ldots;\textbf{assert}(x_{r}\in(\mathcal{A}_{r},\Omega_{r}))$. Note that after the preprocessing, each assertion is of the form $\textbf{assert}(y\in(\mathcal{A},\Omega))$ for a string variable $y$ and a conjunctive FA{} $(\mathcal{A},\Omega)$. Let $S_{0}$ be the resulting program after preprocessing $S$.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@When removing each assignment $y:=f(\vec{x})$ with $\vec{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})$, for each conjunctive FA{} $(\mathcal{A},\Omega)\in\sigma$ (where $\sigma$ is the collective constraints for $y$), nondeterministically guess one disjunct of the pre-image of $f$ under $(\mathcal{A},\Omega)$ (NB.\ here we neither compute the product of the conjunctive FA{}s from $\sigma$, nor compute an explicit representation of the pre-image), say $((\mathcal{A}_{1},\Omega_{1}),\ldots,(\mathcal{A}_{r},\Omega_{r}))$, and insert the sequence of assertions $\textbf{assert}(x_{1}\in(\mathcal{A}_{1},\Omega_{1}));\ldots;\textbf{assert}(x_{r}\in(\mathcal{A}_{r},\Omega_{r}))$ to the program.
\end{itemize}
We then show that the resulting (nondeterministic) decision procedure \emph{requires only exponential space}, which implies the {expspace}{} upper-bound via Savitch's theorem.
\par Let $S^{\prime}$ be the program obtained after removing $y:=f(\vec{x})$ and all assertions with conditions in $\rho$, and $\sigma$ be the set of all conjunctive FA{}s in $\rho$. Then for each $(\mathcal{A},\Omega)\in\sigma$, a disjunct of the conjunctive representation of $\textsf{Pre}_{R_{f}}(\mathcal{A},\Omega)$, say $((\mathcal{A}_{1},\Omega_{1}),\ldots,(\mathcal{A}_{r},\Omega_{r}))$, is guessed, moreover, for each $j\in[r]$, an assertion $\textbf{assert}(x_{j}\in(\mathcal{A}_{j},\Omega_{j}))$ is added. Let $S^{\prime\prime}$ be the resulting program. We say that the assertion $y\in(\mathcal{A},\Omega)$ in $S^{\prime}$ \emph{generates} the assertion $x_{j}\in(\mathcal{A}_{j},\Omega_{j})$ in $S^{\prime\prime}$. One can easily extend this single-step generation relation to multiple steps by considering its transitive closure.
\par Let $S_{1}$ be the resulting program after all the assignments are removed. Namely, $S_{1}$ contains only assertions for input variables.
By induction on the number of removed assignments, we can show that for each input variable $y$ in $S$, each assertion $x\in(\mathcal{A},\Omega)$ in $S_{0}$, and each path $\pi$ from $x$ to $y$ in $G_{S}$, the assertion $x\in(\mathcal{A},\Omega)$ generates \emph{exactly one} assertion $y\in(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})$ in $S_{1}$. Since for each pair of variables $(x,y)$ in $G_{S}$, there are at most exponentially many paths from $x$ to $y$, $S_{1}$ contains at most exponentially many assertions for each input variable. Moreover, according to Lemma~\ref{lem-prerec-comp}, for each assertion $y\in(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})$ in $S_{1}$, suppose that $y\in(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})$ is generated by some assertion $x\in(\mathcal{A},\Omega)$ in $S_{0}$, then $|(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})|$ is at most exponential in $|(\mathcal{A},\Omega)|$. Therefore, we conclude that for each input variable $y$, $S_{1}$ contains at most exponentially many assertions for $y$, where each of them is of at most exponential size.
It follows that the product FA{} of all the assertions for each input variable $y$ in $S_{1}$ is of doubly exponential size.
\par Since the last step of the decision procedure is to decide the nonemptiness of the intersection of all the assertions for each input variable $y$ and nonemptiness of FA{}s can be solved in nondeterministic logarithmic space, we deduce that the last step of the decision procedure can be done in nondeterministic exponential space. We conclude that the aforementioned decision procedure is in nondeterministic exponential space.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:expspace-lower}: Hardness of $\strline[\replaceall]$}
\par We show that the path feasibility problem for $\mathsf{SL}[\mathsf{replaceAll}]$ is $\textsc{expspace}$-hard.
In fact, this holds even when only single characters are replaced.
To show the result, we will give a symbolic execution which ``transforms'' an automaton into exponentially many different automata.
These exponentially many automata will be used to check the vertical tiling relation for a tiling problem with an exponentially wide corridor.
Since such tiling problems are {expspace}-hard, the result follows.
\par After introducing the tiling problem, we will show the form of the symbolic execution without specifying the regular languages used.
Starting from the end of the symbolic execution, we will work backwards, showing the effect that eliminating the $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ and string concatenations has on the regular languages.
We will then show a simple example before giving the precise languages needed to obtain our result.
\par\par\par\par The reduction builds a constraint that is satisfiable only if a given variable $x_{0}$ encodes a solution to a tiling problem.
The solution will be encoded in the following form, for some $h$.
Note, we use different symbols for each bit position of the binary encoding.
We use
$$!\#{0}_{1}\ldots{0}_{n}t^{1}_{1}{0}_{1}\ldots{1}_{n}t^{1}_{2}\ldots{1}_{1}\ldots{1}_{n}t^{1}_{\ell}\#\ldots\#{0}_{1}\ldots{0}_{n}t^{h}_{1}\ldots{1}_{1}\ldots{1}_{n}t^{h}_{\ell}\#!$$
where $t^{i}_{j}$ are tiles, preceded by a binary encoding of the column position of the tile.
The $\#$ character delimits each row and $!$ delimits the ends of the encoding.
It is a standard exercise to express that a string has the above form as the intersection of a polynomial number of polynomially sized automata.
It is also straight-forward to check the horizontal tiling relation using a polynomially sized automaton (each contiguous pair of tiles needs to appear in the horizontal relation $H$).
\par The difficulty lies in asserting that the vertical tiling relation $V$ is respected.
For a given position
$b_{1}\ldots b_{n}$,
it is easy to construct an automaton that checks the tiles in the column labelled
$b_{1}\ldots b_{n}$
respects $V$.
The crux of our reduction is showing that, using only concatenation and replaceall, we can transform a single automaton constraint into an exponential number of checks, one for each position
$b_{1}\ldots b_{n}$.
Recall, concatenation can be expressed using $\mathsf{replaceAll}$.
\par This process will be explained in full shortly, but first let us give a simple example of how we can obtain the required checks.
\par\begin{example}Let $n=2$ and $V=\{(t_{1},t_{2}),(t_{2},t_{1})\}$.
\par Consider the constraint (which we will explain in the sequel)
$$\begin{array}[]{c}y_{1}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{0}_{1}}(x_{0},\$^{0}_{1});\ \ z_{1}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{1}_{1}}(x_{0},\$^{1}_{1});\\
x_{1}:=y_{1}\circ z_{1};\\
y_{2}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{0}_{2}}(x_{1},\$^{0}_{2});\ \ z_{2}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{1}_{2}}(x_{1},\$^{1}_{2});\\
x_{2}:=y_{2}\circ z_{2};\\
x_{2}\in\mathcal{A}_{V}\end{array}$$where $\mathcal{A}_{V}$ is the automaton below.
The purpose of this automaton will become clear later in the description.
\begin{figure*}\includegraphics[scale]{expspace-hardness-example}
\end{figure*}\par\par\par Let
$x_{0}$ take the value
$$!\#{0}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{1}{0}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{1}{1}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{2}{1}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{2}\ \#\ {0}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{2}{0}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{2}{1}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{1}{1}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{1}\#!.$$That is, there are two rows in $x_{0}$, separated by $\#$, and in each row, $x_{0}$ counts from $00$ to $11$ in binary (marked with the bit indices $1,2$).
After the first pair of $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ operations and concatenation of $y_{1}$ and $z_{1}$, the variable $x_{1}$ must have the value
$$\begin{array}[]{l}!\#\$^{0}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{1}\$^{0}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{1}{1}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{2}{1}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{2}\ \#\ \$^{0}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{2}\$^{0}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{2}{1}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{1}{1}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{1}\#!\\
!\#{0}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{1}{0}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{1}\$^{1}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{2}\$^{1}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{2}\ \#\ {0}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{2}{0}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{2}\$^{1}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{1}\$^{1}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{1}\#!\end{array}$$After the next $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ operations and concatenation of $y_{2}$ and $z_{2}$, the variable $x_{2}$ has the value
$$\begin{array}[]{l}!\#\underline{\$^{0}_{1}\$^{0}_{2}t_{1}}\$^{0}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{1}{1}_{1}\$^{0}_{2}t_{2}{1}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{2}\ \#\ \underline{\$^{0}_{1}\$^{0}_{2}t_{2}}\$^{0}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{2}{1}_{1}\$^{0}_{2}t_{1}{1}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{1}\#!\\
!\#{0}_{1}\$^{0}_{2}t_{1}{0}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{1}\underline{\$^{1}_{1}\$^{0}_{2}t_{2}}\$^{1}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{2}\ \#\ {0}_{1}\$^{0}_{2}t_{2}{0}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{2}\underline{\$^{1}_{1}\$^{0}_{2}t_{1}}\$^{1}_{1}{1}_{2}t_{1}\#!\\
!\#\$^{0}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{1}\underline{\$^{0}_{1}\$^{1}_{2}t_{1}}{1}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{2}{1}_{1}\$^{1}_{2}t_{2}\ \#\ \$^{0}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{2}\underline{\$^{0}_{1}\$^{1}_{2}t_{2}}{1}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{1}{1}_{1}\$^{1}_{2}t_{1}\#!\\
!\#{0}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{1}{0}_{1}\$^{1}_{2}t_{1}\$^{1}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{2}\underline{\$^{1}_{1}\$^{1}_{2}t_{2}}\ \#\ {0}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{2}{0}_{1}\$^{1}_{2}t_{2}\$^{1}_{1}{0}_{2}t_{1}\underline{\$^{1}_{1}\$^{1}_{2}t_{1}}\#!\end{array}$$Notice that this value has four (altered) copies of the original value of $x_{0}$ and this value enjoys the property that each copy of $x_{0}$ contains the occurrences of exactly one of $\$^{0}_{1}\$^{0}_{2}$, $\$^{1}_{1}\$^{0}_{2}$, $\$^{0}_{1}\$^{1}_{2}$, $\$^{1}_{1}\$^{1}_{2}$.
In each copy of $x_{0}$, we have underlined the positions where the vertical tiling relation is checked.
In particular, in the first copy, the vertical tiling relation is checked for the ${0}_{1}{0}_{2}$ position of $x_{0}$, witnessed by the run of $\mathcal{A}_{V}$ on the first copy sketched below,
$$\begin{array}[]{l}q_{0}\xrightarrow{!}q_{0}\xrightarrow{\#}q_{1}\xrightarrow{\$^{0}_{1}}q_{2}\xrightarrow{\$^{0}_{2}}q_{3}\xrightarrow{t_{1}}(q_{1},t_{1})\ldots(p_{3},t_{1})\xrightarrow{t_{2}}(q_{1},t_{1})\xrightarrow{\#}(q_{1},t_{1})\\
\xrightarrow{\$^{0}_{1}}(q_{2},t_{1})\xrightarrow{\$^{0}_{2}}(q_{3},t_{1})\xrightarrow{t_{2}}(q_{1},t_{2})\ldots(q_{1},t_{2})\xrightarrow{\#}(q_{1},t_{2})\xrightarrow{!}q_{0}.\end{array}$$In the next copies, the vertical tiling relation is checked for the positions
${1}_{1}{0}_{2}$,
${0}_{1}{1}_{2}$, and
${1}_{1}{1}_{2}$
in $x_{0}$ respectively. Note that $\mathcal{A}_{V}$ \emph{does not} have to check that in each copy, the same position in each row is marked (which would need a state space exponential in $n$).
In particular, the occurrences of the subwords $\$^{b_{1}}_{1}\$^{b_{2}}_{2}$ ($b_{1},b_{2}\in\{0,1\}$) are equal in each copy since the value of $x_{2}$ resulting from the assignments already guarantees this. \qed\end{example}
\par\par\par\par\par\par We now give the formal proof. Fix a tiling problem with
tiles $\Theta$,
initial and final tiles $t_{I}$ and $t_{F}$ respectively,
horizontal and vertical tiling relations $H$ and $V$,
and a width $\ell=2^{n}$ for some $n$.
We will create a symbolic execution in $\mathsf{SL}[\mathsf{replaceAll}]$ which is path feasible iff the tiling problem has a solution.
\par In particular, we will require that a certain variable $x_{1}$ can take a value
$$!\#{0}_{1}\ldots{0}_{n}t^{1}_{1}{0}_{1}\ldots{1}_{n}t^{1}_{2}\ldots{1}_{1}\ldots{1}_{n}t^{1}_{\ell}\#\ldots\#{0}_{1}\ldots{0}_{n}t^{h}_{1}\ldots{1}_{1}\ldots{1}_{n}t^{h}_{\ell}\#!$$
where
$!,\#,{0}_{1},{1}_{1},\ldots,{0}_{n},{1}_{n}\notin\Theta$.
In particular, the string encodes a solution to the tiling problem, where each tile is preceded by a binary number of length $n$ representing the number of the column in which it appears.
Note, we have a different character for each bit position, e.g. ${0}_{2}$ is a distinct character from ${0}_{3}$.
The $!$ will be used to mark the beginning and end of the string and $\#$ is used to separate the rows of the solution.
\par To reach such a solution, we will use the symbolic execution to effectively generate an exponential number of languages.
We can number these languages in binary, i.e.\ $L_{0\ldots 00}$ to $L_{1\ldots 11}$.
In addition to $!$, $\#$, ${0}_{i}$, and ${1}_{i}$, we will also introduce characters of the form
$\$^{b^{\prime}}_{i,b}$.
In language
$L_{b_{1}\ldots b_{n}}$,
the character
$\$^{b^{\prime}}_{i,b}$
will mean that if $b_{i}=b$, then match the character
$b^{\prime}\in\{{0}_{i},{1}_{i}\}$.
This will become clearer when we show an example, but first we will give the symbolic execution needed to generate the languages.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{The symbolic execution}
\par$$\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varphi&=&\text{{assert}($x_{1}\in L$)};\ \text{{assert}($x_{1}\in L_{H}$)};\\
&&\text{{assert}($x_{1}\in L_{1}$)};\ldots;\text{{assert}($x_{1}\in L_{n}$)};\\
\\
&&y^{0}_{2}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{0}_{1}}(x_{1},\$^{0}_{1,0});\\
&&y^{1}_{2}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{1}_{1}}(y^{0}_{2},\$^{1}_{1,0});\\
&&z^{0}_{2}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{0}_{1}}(x_{1},\$^{0}_{1,1});\\
&&z^{1}_{2}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{1}_{1}}(z^{0}_{2},\$^{1}_{1,1});\\
&&x_{2}:=y^{1}_{2}\circ z^{1}_{2};\\
\\
&&y^{0}_{3}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{0}_{2}}(x_{2},\$^{0}_{2,0});\\
&&y^{1}_{3}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{1}_{2}}(y^{0}_{3},\$^{1}_{2,0});\\
&&z^{0}_{3}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{0}_{2}}(x_{2},\$^{0}_{2,1});\\
&&z^{1}_{3}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{1}_{2}}(z^{0}_{2},\$^{1}_{2,1});\\
&&x_{3}:=y^{1}_{3}\circ z^{1}_{3};\\
\\
&&\ldots\\
&&y^{0}_{n}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{0}_{n}}(x_{n-1},\$^{0}_{n,0});\\
&&y^{1}_{n}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{1}_{n}}(y^{0}_{n},\$^{1}_{n,0});\\
&&z^{0}_{n}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{0}_{n}}(x_{n-1},\$^{0}_{n,1});\\
&&z^{1}_{n}:=\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{1}_{n}}(z^{0}_{n},\$^{1}_{n,1});\\
&&x_{n}:=y^{1}_{n}\circ z^{1}_{n};\\
\\
&&\text{{assert}($x_{n}\in L_{V}$)}\end{array}$$
\par The symbolic execution we will need to encode solutions to the tiling problem is put above.
The regular constraints on $x_{1}$ will enforce that any solution also gives a solution to the tiling problem if we do not enforce the vertical matching relation.
To enforce the vertical matching relation we use the constraint on $x_{n}$ at the end.
Notice that each sequence of uses of $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ translates between
$\$^{b^{\prime}}_{i,b}$
and the values ${0}_{i}$ and ${1}_{i}$, where the $y$ variables handle the case where $b$ is $0$, and the $z$ when $b$ is $1$.
This process will be illuminated later with an example.
Note, $\circ$ can be expressed with $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ and hence is not directly needed.
\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Unravelling the Constraint}
\par We show how $\varphi$ can lead to $x_{1}$ having to be included in an exponential number of languages.
To do this, we eliminate each use of $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ and $\circ$ from the bottom-up.
The process is illustrated in the forwards direction by the example in Section~\ref{sec:simple-expspace-example}.
However, note, in Section~\ref{sec:simple-expspace-example} we did not use the characters
$\$^{b^{\prime}}_{i,b}$
but a simpler version
$\$^{b}_{i}$.
After our explanation, we give a similar example for the backwards direction.
\par The first step is to eliminate
$x_{n}:=y^{1}_{n}\circ z^{1}_{n}$.
This is done by removing
$x_{n}:=y^{1}_{n}\circ z^{1}_{n}$.
and replacing it with
$\text{{assert}($y^{1}_{n}\in L^{\prime}_{0}$)};\text{{assert}($z^{1}_{n}\in L^{\prime}_{1}$)}$
where $L_{V}=L^{\prime}_{0}\circ L^{\prime}_{1}$.
This can be done by taking an automaton $\mathcal{A}$ such that $L_{V}=\mathcal{L}\mathord{\left(\mathcal{A}\right)}$ and guessing the state at the split between $y^{1}_{n}$ and $z^{1}_{n}$ in an accepting run over the value of $x_{n}$.
This means that $L^{\prime}_{0}$ and $L^{\prime}_{1}$ can be represented by automata with the same states and transitions, but different initial and final states.
\par Next, we eliminate the $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ functions.
This leads to
$\text{{assert}($x_{n-1}\in L_{0}$)}$,
where $L_{0}$ is $L^{\prime}_{0}$ except all $\$^{b}_{n,0}$ characters have been replaced by ${b}_{n}$.
Similarly, we also have
$\text{{assert}($x_{n-1}\in L_{1}$)}$,
where $L_{1}$ is $L^{\prime}_{1}$ except all $\$^{b}_{n,1}$ characters have been replaced by ${b}_{n}$.
Note that different $\$^{b}_{n,b^{\prime}}$ characters have been replaced in $L_{0}$ and $L_{1}$.
The languages have begun to diverge.
\par We then eliminate
$x_{n-1}:=y^{1}_{n-1}\circ z^{1}_{n-1}$.
Thus $L_{0}$ needs to be split into $L^{\prime}_{00}$ and $L^{\prime}_{10}$.
Similarly, $L_{1}$ needs to be split into $L^{\prime}_{01}$ and $L^{\prime}_{11}$.
This results in the constraints
$$\text{{assert}($y^{1}_{n-1}\in L^{\prime}_{00}$)};\text{{assert}($y^{1}_{n-1}\in L^{\prime}_{01}$)};\text{{assert}($z^{1}_{n-1}\in L^{\prime}_{10}$)};\text{{assert}($z^{1}_{n-1}\in L^{\prime}_{11}$)}$$
and after eliminating the next batch of $\mathsf{replaceAll}$ functions we have
$$\text{{assert}($y^{1}_{n-1}\in L_{00}$)};\text{{assert}($y^{1}_{n-1}\in L_{01}$)};\text{{assert}($z^{1}_{n-1}\in L_{10}$)};\text{{assert}($z^{1}_{n-1}\in L_{11}$)}\ .$$
\par By following this procedure, we eventually obtain the constraints
$$\text{{assert}($x_{1}\in L_{0\ldots 00}$)};\text{{assert}($x_{1}\in L_{0\ldots 01}$)};\ldots;\text{{assert}($x_{1}\in L_{1\ldots 11}$)}\ .$$
Notice, furthermore, that
$L_{b_{1}\ldots b_{n}}$
has all
$\$^{0}_{i,b_{i}}$
replaced by ${0}_{i}$
and all
$\$^{1}_{i,b_{i}}$
replaced by ${1}_{i}$
but all characters
$\$^{b}_{i,b^{\prime}_{i}}$
where
$b^{\prime}_{i}\neq b_{i}$
are unchanged.
\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Controlling the Unravelling}
\par We revisit the example from Section~\ref{sec:simple-expspace-example} for the case of $n=3$ to show how we can use the unravelling to obtain automata which will be useful to our encoding.
In this example, we will ignore the issue of initial and final states, and just show the effect on the automaton transition relation.
Let $n=3$ and $L_{V}$ be defined by the automaton below.
\begin{center}\hbox to196.12pt{\vbox to58.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.70001pt\lower-29.3221pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\par We initially have
$\text{{assert}($x_{3}\in L_{V}$)}$.
We first eliminate
$x_{3}:=y^{1}_{3}\circ z^{1}_{3}$ and then
$$\begin{array}[]{rcl}y^{0}_{3}&:=&\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{0}_{3}}(x_{2},\$^{0}_{3,0});\\
y^{1}_{3}&:=&\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{1}_{3}}(y^{0}_{3},\$^{1}_{3,0});\\
z^{0}_{3}&:=&\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{0}_{3}}(x_{2},\$^{0}_{3,1});\\
z^{1}_{3}&:=&\mathsf{replaceAll}_{{1}_{3}}(z^{0}_{3},\$^{1}_{3,1})\ .\end{array}$$
Note, from $L^{\prime}_{0}$ we replace the $\$^{0}_{3,0}$- and $\$^{1}_{3,0}$-transitions (although the latter does not appear in $\mathcal{A}$), while from $L^{\prime}_{1}$ we replace the $\$^{0}_{3,1}$- and $\$^{1}_{3,1}$-transitions.
This leaves us with
$$\text{{assert}($x_{2}\in L_{0}$)};\text{{assert}($\land x_{2}\in L_{1}$)}$$
where $L_{0}$ is given by

\end{center}
If we further insist that $x_{1}$ contains only characters from
$\{{0}_{1},{1}_{1},{1}_{2},{1}_{2},{1}_{3},{1}_{3}\}$
then the only path from $q_{0}$ to $q_{3}$ is via the sequence
${0}_{1}{1}_{2}{0}_{3}$.
Likewise, in $L_{111}$ the only valid sequence will be
${1}_{1}{1}_{2}{1}_{3}$.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Completing the Reduction}
\par To finish the reduction, we have to instantiate $\varphi$ by giving
$L$, $L_{H}$, $L_{1}$, \ldots, $L_{n}$ and $L_{V}$ where all languages but $L_{V}$ are easily seen to be representable by automata whose size is polynomial in $n$.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$L$ will enforce that $x_{1}$ matches
$$!\left(\#\left(\{{0}_{1},{1}_{1}\}\ldots\{{0}_{n},{1}_{n}\}\Theta\right)^{\ast}\right)^{\ast}\#!\ .$$That is, a sequence of delimited rows, each consisting of a sequence of alternating $n$-bit sequences and tiles;
the beginning and end of the word is marked by $!$.
Moreover, $L$ will insist that the first tile seen is $t_{I}$ and that the final tile seen is $t_{F}$, as required by the tiling problem.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$L_{H}$ will enforce that any subsequence
$$tb_{1}\ldots b_{n}t^{\prime}$$where for all $i$ we have
$b_{i}\in\{{0}_{i},{1}_{i}\}$
in the value of $x_{1}$ is such that $\left(t,t^{\prime}\right)\in H$.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@The languages $L_{1},\ldots L_{n}$ will together enforce the correct sequencing of bit values $b_{1}\ldots b_{n}$.
That is, between each $\#$ each sequence $b_{1}\ldots b_{n}$ appears exactly once and in the correct order
(i.e.\ ${0}_{1}\ldots{0}_{n-1}{0}_{n}$
appears before
${0}_{1}\ldots{0}_{n-1}{1}_{n}$
and so on up to
${1}_{1}\ldots{1}_{n-1}{1}_{n}$).
To do this, each $L_{i}$ will check the following.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@After each $\#$ the first instance of
$\{{0}_{i},{1}_{i}\}$
is ${0}_{i}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Before each $\#$ the last instance of
$\{{0}_{i},{1}_{i}\}$
is ${1}_{i}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@For every ${0}_{i}$ such that
$\#$ does not appear before the next occurrence of
$b_{i}\in\{{0}_{i},{1}_{i}\}$,
if the immediately succeeding characters are
${1}_{i+1}\ldots{1}_{n}$
then $b_{i}$ is ${1}_{i}$,
otherwise $b_{i}$ is ${0}_{i}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@For every ${1}_{i}$ such that
$\#$ does not appear before the next occurrence of
$b_{i}\in\{{0}_{i},{1}_{i}\}$,
if the immediately succeeding characters are
${1}_{i+1}\ldots{1}_{n}$
then $b_{i}$ is ${0}_{i}$,
otherwise $b_{i}$ is ${1}_{i}$.
\end{enumerate}\end{enumerate}
\par Finally, we need to define $L_{V}$.
As seen above, $L_{V}$ will lead to an exponential number of languages
$L_{b_{1}\ldots b_{n}}$
inside which $x_{1}$ will need to be contained.
The role of
$L_{b_{1}\ldots b_{n}}$
will be to check that the
$b_{1}\ldots b_{n}$th
column of the tiling obeys the vertical matching relation.
Each
$L_{b_{1}\ldots b_{n}}$
can be seen to be representable by a polynomially sized automaton that stores in its states the last tile seen after the sequence
$b_{1}\ldots b_{n}$.
Then, when the sequence next occurs, the new tile can be compared with the previous one.
The automaton will proceed by storing the new tile and forgetting the old.
\par We will design an automaton for $L_{V}$ which will lead to the generation of the correct automaton for each
$L_{b_{1}\ldots b_{n}}$.
We will use characters
$\$^{b^{\prime}}_{i,b}$
as before to generate the required bit sequences in the transition labelling of the automaton representing
$L_{b_{1}\ldots b_{n}}$.
